**France**,[^cite_note-20] officially the **French Republic**,[^cite_note-21] is a country primarily located in [Western Europe](33800.md).[^cite_note-22] [Its overseas regions and territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_France) include [French Guiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Guiana) in [South America](26769.md), [Saint Pierre and Miquelon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Pierre_and_Miquelon) in the [North Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic), the [French West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_West_Indies), and [many islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_France) in [Oceania](22621.md) and the [Indian Ocean](14580.md). [Metropolitan France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_France) shares borders with [Belgium](3343.md) and [Luxembourg](17515.md) to the north; [Germany](11867.md) to the northeast; [Switzerland](26748.md) to the east; [Italy](14532.md) and [Monaco](19261.md) to the southeast; [Andorra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andorra) and [Spain](26667.md) to the south; and a maritime border with the [United Kingdom](31717.md) to the northwest. Its metropolitan area extends from the [Rhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine) to the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) and from the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to the [English Channel](9230.md) and the [North Sea](21179.md). Its 18 [integral regions](58846.md#List_of_administrative_regions)—five of which are overseas—span a combined area of 632,702 km<sup>2</sup> (244,288 sq mi), with [a total population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_France) estimated at over 69.1 million in 2026. Its capital, [largest city](76050805.md) and main cultural and economic centre is [Paris](22989.md), with a metropolitan population of over 13 million.&#x20;

Metropolitan France was settled during the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age) by [Celtic tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ancient_Celtic_peoples_and_tribes) known as [Gauls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauls) before [Rome annexed the area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Gaul) in 51 BC, leading to a distinct [Gallo-Roman culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallo-Roman_culture). In the [Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages), the [Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franks) formed the kingdom of [Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francia), which became the heartland of the [Carolingian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolingian_Empire). The [Treaty of Verdun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Verdun) of 843 partitioned the empire, with [West Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Francia) evolving into the [Kingdom of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France). In the [High Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Middle_Ages), France was a powerful but decentralised [feudal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) kingdom, but after [Philip II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_France) defeated the [Angevin Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angevin_Empire) and won the [Battle of Bouvines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bouvines), it became more centralized and became the most powerful country in Europe. From the mid-14th to the mid-15th centuries, France was plunged into a dynastic conflict with [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) known as the [Hundred Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Years%27_War). In the 16th century, [French culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_France) flourished during the [French Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Renaissance), and a [French colonial empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonial_empire) emerged. Internally, France was dominated by the conflict with the [House of Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Habsburg) and the [French Wars of Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Wars_of_Religion) between [Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholics) and [Huguenots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huguenots). France was successful in the [Thirty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years%27_War) and further increased its influence during the reign of [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV). Under his rule, the nation built Europe's strongest army, fought a series of wars, and experienced artistic and scientific flourishing.&#x20;

The [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution) of 1789 overthrew the *[ancien régime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancien_r%C3%A9gime)* and produced the [Declaration of the Rights of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen), which expresses the nation's ideals to this day. France reached its political and military zenith in the early 19th century under [Napoleon Bonaparte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon), subjugating much of continental Europe and establishing the [First French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_French_Empire). Its collapse initiated a period of relative decline during which France endured the [Bourbon Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Restoration_in_France) until the founding of the [French Second Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Second_Republic), which was succeeded by the [Second French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_French_Empire) upon [Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III)'s takeover. His empire collapsed during the [Franco-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Prussian_War) in 1870. This led to the establishment of the [French Third Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Third_Republic), as well as a period of economic prosperity and cultural and scientific flourishing known as the [Belle Époque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belle_%C3%89poque). France was one of the [major participants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_Entente) of [World War I](4764461.md), from which [it emerged victorious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles) at great human and economic cost. It was among the [Allies of World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II), but it surrendered and [was occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_military_administration_in_occupied_France_during_World_War_II) by [Germany](21212.md) in 1940. Following [its liberation in 1944](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_of_France), the short-lived [Fourth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Fourth_Republic) was established and later dissolved over the course of the [Algerian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_War). The current [Fifth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Fifth_Republic), a [semi-presidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_system) system, was formed in 1958 by [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle). [Algeria](358.md) and most French colonies became independent in the 1960s, with the majority retaining [close economic and military ties with France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7afrique).&#x20;

France retains its centuries-long status as a global centre [of art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_art), [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_France), [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_cuisine) and [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_philosophy). It hosts the [fourth-largest number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites_by_country) of UNESCO [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites), with [54 in total](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_France), and is the [world's leading tourist destination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_rankings), having received 102 million foreign [visitors in 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_France). A [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country), France has a [high nominal per capita GDP globally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita), and [its economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_France) ranks among the largest in the world [by both nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) and [PPP-adjusted GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)). It is a [great power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_power), being one of the five [permanent members of the United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_members_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council) and an official [nuclear-weapon state](400821.md). The country is part of [multiple international organisations and forums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outline_of_France#International_organisation_membership).&#x20;

## Etymology

Main article: [Name of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_France)

Originally applied to the whole [Frankish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francia), the name *France* comes from the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) *[Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francia)*, or 'realm of the [Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franks)'.[^cite_note-23] The [name of the Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_the_Franks) is related to the English word *frank* ('free'): the latter stems from the [Old French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_French) *franc* ('free, noble, sincere') and ultimately from the [Medieval Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Latin) word *francus* ('free, exempt from service; a freeman, a Frank'), a generalisation of the tribal name that emerged as a [Late Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Latin) borrowing of the reconstructed [Frankish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankish_language) [endonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endonym_and_exonym) \**Frank*.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-OnlineEtymologyDictionary-25] It has been suggested that the meaning 'free' was adopted because after the conquest of [Gaul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaul), only Franks were free of taxation[^cite_note-26] or, more generally, because they had the status of freemen in contrast to servants or slaves.[^cite_note-OnlineEtymologyDictionary-25] The etymology of *\*Frank* is uncertain. It is traditionally derived from the [Proto-Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Germanic_language) word \**frankōn*, which translates to 'javelin' or 'lance' (the throwing axe of the Franks was known as the *[francisca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisca)*),[^cite_note-27] although these weapons may have been named because of their use by the Franks, not the other way around.[^cite_note-OnlineEtymologyDictionary-25]&#x20;

In English, 'France' is pronounced [/fræns/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*FRANSS*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key) in American English and [/frɑːns/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*FRAHNSS*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key) or [/fræns/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*FRANSS*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key) in British English. The pronunciation with [/ɑː/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) is mostly confined to accents with the [trap–bath split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trap%E2%80%93bath_split), such as [Received Pronunciation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Received_Pronunciation), though it can be also heard in some other dialects, such as [Cardiff English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiff_English).[^cite_note-28]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_France)

### Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistory of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_France)

The oldest traces of archaic humans in what is now France date from approximately 1.8 million years ago.[^cite_note-Jean_Carpentier_1987_p.17-29] [Neanderthals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal) occupied the region into the [Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic) era but were slowly replaced by *[Homo sapiens](682482.md)* around 35,000 BC.[^cite_note-30] This period witnessed the emergence of [cave painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_painting) in the [Dordogne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dordogne) and [Pyrenees](24707.md), including at [Lascaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lascaux), dated to c. 18,000 BC.[^cite_note-Jean_Carpentier_1987_p.17-29] At the end of the [Last Glacial Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period) (10,000 BC), the climate became milder.[^cite_note-Jean_Carpentier_1987_p.17-29] From approximately 7,000 BC, this part of Western Europe entered the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) era, and its inhabitants became [sedentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedentism).&#x20;

After demographic and agricultural development between the 4th and 3rd millennia BC, [metallurgy appeared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-age_system), initially working gold, [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcolithic) and [bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age), then later [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age).[^cite_note-31] France has numerous [megalithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalith) sites from the Neolithic, including the [Carnac stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnac_stones) site (approximately 3,300 BC).&#x20;

### Antiquity (6th century BC – 5th century AD)

In 600 BC, [Ionian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionia) [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_pre-Roman_Gaul) from [Phocaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phocaea) founded the colony of [Massalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massalia) (present-day [Marseille](40888948.md)).[^cite_note-32] [Celtic tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_tribes) penetrated parts of eastern and northern France, spreading through the rest of the country between the 5th and 3rd century BC.[^cite_note-33] Around 390 BC, the Gallic chieftain [Brennus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brennus_\(leader_of_the_Senones\)) and his troops made their way to [Roman Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Italy), defeated the Romans in the [Battle of the Allia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Allia), and besieged and ransomed Rome.[^cite_note-34] This left Rome weakened, and the Gauls continued to harass the region until 345 BC, when they entered into a peace treaty.[^cite_note-35] However, the Romans and the Gauls remained adversaries for centuries.[^cite_note-36]&#x20;

Maison Carrée temple in Nemausus Corinthian columns and portico

The [Maison carrée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maison_carr%C3%A9e) was a temple of the [Gallo-Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallo-Roman_culture) city of [Nemausus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nemausus) (present-day [Nîmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C3%AEmes)) and is one of the best-preserved [Roman temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_temple) anywhere.

Around 125 BC, the south of Gaul was conquered by the Romans, who called this region *[Provincia Nostra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallia_Narbonensis)* ("Our Province"), which evolved into [Provence](48503.md) in French.[^cite_note-37] [Julius Caesar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Caesar) conquered the remainder of Gaul and overcame a revolt by Gallic chieftain [Vercingetorix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vercingetorix) in 52 BC.[^cite_note-38] Gaul was divided by [Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus) into provinces,[^cite_note-c53-39] and many cities were founded during the [Gallo-Roman period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Gaul), including [Lugdunum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lugdunum) (present-day [Lyon](8638634.md)), the capital of the Gauls.[^cite_note-c53-39] In 250–290 AD, Roman Gaul suffered a crisis with its [fortified borders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limes_\(Roman_Empire\)) attacked by barbarians.[^cite_note-c77-40] The situation improved in the first half of the 4th century, a period of revival and prosperity.[^cite_note-41] In 312, Emperor [Constantine I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great) [converted to Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great_and_Christianity). The Christian demographic, who had been persecuted, increased.[^cite_note-42] However, the onset of the 5th century marked the resumption of major [tribal migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_Period).[^cite_note-43] [Teutonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teutons) tribes invaded the region, the [Visigoths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigoths) settling in the southwest, the [Burgundians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burgundians) along the [Rhine Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine_Valley), and the Franks in the north.[^cite_note-44]&#x20;

### Early Middle Ages (5th–10th century)

In [late antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_antiquity), Gaul was divided into Germanic kingdoms and a remaining Gallo-Roman territory. [Celtic Britons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Britons), fleeing the [Anglo-Saxon settlement of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_settlement_of_Britain), settled in west [Armorica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armorica); the Armorican peninsula was renamed [Brittany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brittany), and [Celtic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celts) was revived.&#x20;

The first leader to unite all Franks was [Clovis I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clovis_I), who began his reign as king of the [Salian Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salian_Franks) in 481, routing the last forces of the Roman governors in 486. Clovis said he would be baptised a Christian in the event of victory against the [Visigothic Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigothic_Kingdom), which was said to have guaranteed the battle. Clovis [regained the southwest from the Visigoths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Visigothic_Wars) and was baptised in 508. Clovis was the first [Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples) conqueror after the [Fall of the Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire) to convert to Catholic Christianity; thus, France was given the title "Eldest daughter of the Church" by the papacy,[^cite_note-45] and French kings were called "the Most Christian Kings of France".&#x20;

painting of Clovis I conversion to Catholicism in 498, a king being baptised in a tub in a cathedral surrounded by bishop and monks

With [Clovis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clovis_I)'s conversion to Catholicism in 498, the [Frankish monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Frankish_kings), [elective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elective_monarchy) and [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) until then, became [hereditary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hereditary_monarchy) and of [divine right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_right_of_kings).

The Franks embraced the Christian [Gallo-Roman culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallo-Roman_culture), and Gaul was renamed *[Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francia)* ("Land of the Franks"). The Germanic Franks adopted [Romanic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_languages). Clovis made [Paris](22989.md) his capital and established the [Merovingian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merovingian_dynasty), but his kingdom would not survive his death. The Franks treated land as a private possession and divided it among their heirs, so four kingdoms emerged from that of Clovis: Paris, [Orléans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orl%C3%A9ans), [Soissons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soissons), and [Rheims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reims). The [last Merovingian kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roi_fain%C3%A9ant) [lost power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_behind_the_throne) to their [mayors of the palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_palace) (head of household). One mayor of the palace, [Charles Martel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Martel), defeated an [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) invasion at the [Battle of Tours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tours) in 732.[^cite_note-46] His son, [Pepin the Short](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pepin_the_Short), seized the crown of Francia from the weakened Merovingians and founded the [Carolingian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolingian_dynasty). Pepin's son [Charlemagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne) reunited the Frankish kingdoms and built an empire across Western and [Central Europe](5188.md).&#x20;

Proclaimed [Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor) by [Pope Leo III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Leo_III) and thus establishing the French government's longtime [historical association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Catholic_Church_in_France) with the [Catholic Church](606848.md),[^cite_note-georgetown1-47] Charlemagne tried to revive the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire) and its cultural grandeur. Charlemagne's son [Louis I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_the_Pious) kept the empire united, although it was divided between Louis' three sons into [East Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Francia), [Middle Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Francia) and [West Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Francia) in 843. West Francia approximated the area occupied by modern France and was its precursor.[^cite_note-48]&#x20;

Throughout the 9th and 10th centuries, incessant [Viking raids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking_expansion) severely weakened the Carolingian monarchy, accelerating the fragmentation of West Francia into a decentralised, [feudal society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism). As central authority faltered, local nobility secured hereditary rights to their titles and lands, creating a system where the king's authority became more religious and symbolic than secular, frequently challenged by powerful territorial princes. After the [Battle of Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Hastings) in 1066, [William the Conqueror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_the_Conqueror) added "King of England" to his titles, becoming vassal and the equal of the king of France, creating recurring tensions.&#x20;

### High and Late Middle Ages (10th–15th century)

See also: [France in the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_in_the_Middle_Ages)



[Joan of Arc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_of_Arc) led the [French Army](611740.md) to several important victories during the [Hundred Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Years%27_War) (1337–1453), which paved the way for the final victory.

The Carolingian dynasty ruled France until 987, when [Hugh Capet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Capet) was crowned [king of the Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_French_monarchs).[^cite_note-49] His descendants unified the country through wars and inheritance. From 1190, the [Capetian rulers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capetian_dynasty) began to be referred as "kings of France" rather than "kings of the Franks".[^cite_note-50] Later kings expanded their directly possessed [*domaine royal*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_lands_of_France) to cover over half of modern France by the 15th century. Royal authority became more assertive, centred on a [hierarchically conceived society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estates_of_the_realm) distinguishing [nobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_nobility), clergy, and [commoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estates_General_\(France\)).&#x20;

The nobility played a prominent role in [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades) to restore Christian access to the [Holy Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land). French knights made up most reinforcements in the 200 years of the Crusades, in such a fashion that the Arabs referred to crusaders as *Franj*.[^cite_note-google.fr-51] French Crusaders imported French into the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant), making [Old French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_French) the base of the *[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)* ("Frankish language") of the [Crusader states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusader_states).[^cite_note-google.fr-51] The [Albigensian Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albigensian_Crusade) was launched in 1209 to eliminate the heretical [Cathars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catharism) in the southwest of modern-day France.[^cite_note-52]&#x20;

From the 11th century, the [House of Plantagenet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Plantagenet), rulers of the [County of Anjou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_of_Anjou), established its dominion over the surrounding provinces of [Maine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_\(province\)) and [Touraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Touraine), then built an "empire" from England to the [Pyrenees](24707.md), covering half of modern France. Tensions between France and the [Plantagenet empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angevin_Empire) would last a hundred years, until [Philip II of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_France) conquered most of its continental possessions between 1202 and 1214 and defeated the Angevin coalition at the [Battle of Bouvines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bouvines), leaving only England and [Aquitaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquitaine) to the Plantagenets. Following this victory, France became the most prosperous and powerful kingdom in Europe.[^cite_note-53]&#x20;

[Charles IV the Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_IV_of_France) died without an heir in 1328.[^cite_note-guerard-54] The crown passed to [Philip of Valois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_VI_of_France) rather than Edward Plantagenet, who had become [Edward III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_III) of England the previous year. During the reign of Philip, the monarchy reached the height of its medieval power.[^cite_note-guerard-54] Edward contested Philip's throne in 1337, beginning the intermittent [Hundred Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Years%27_War) between England and France.[^cite_note-55] Boundaries changed, but landholdings inside France by English kings remained extensive for decades. With charismatic leaders such as [Joan of Arc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_of_Arc), French counterattacks won back most English continental territories. France was struck by the [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death), from which half of the 17 million population died.[^cite_note-56]&#x20;

### Early modern period (15th century–1789)

Main article: [France in the early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_in_the_early_modern_period)

The [French Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Renaissance) saw cultural development and standardisation of French, which became the [official language of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinance_of_Villers-Cotter%C3%AAts) and Europe's aristocracy. France became rivals of the [House of Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Habsburg) during the [Italian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Wars), which would dictate much of their later foreign policy until the mid-18th century. French explorers claimed lands in the Americas, paving expansion of the [French colonial empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonial_empire). The rise of Protestantism led France to a series of civil wars known as the [French Wars of Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Wars_of_Religion).[^cite_note-57] [Huguenots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huguenots) subsequently emigrated in significant numbers to Protestant regions including [England](9316.md), the [Dutch Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Republic), and German Protestant states. The wars were ended by [Henri IV's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_IV_of_France) [Edict of Nantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Nantes), which granted some freedom of religion to the Huguenots. [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_Spain) troops[^cite_note-58] assisted the Catholics from 1589 to 1594 and invaded France in 1597. Spain and France returned to all-out war between 1635 and 1659. The [Franco-Spanish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Spanish_War_\(1635%E2%80%931659\)) cost France 300,000 casualties; total deaths on both sides were 200,000.[^cite_note-c-59]&#x20;

Under [Louis XIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIII), [Cardinal Richelieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardinal_Richelieu) promoted centralisation of the state and reinforced royal power. He destroyed castles of defiant lords and denounced the use of private armies. By the end of the 1620s, Richelieu established "the royal monopoly of force".[^cite_note-60] France fought in the [Thirty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years%27_War), supporting the Protestant side against the Habsburgs. From the 16th to the 19th century, France was responsible for about 10% of the [transatlantic slave trade](49005.md).[^cite_note-BNF-61]&#x20;

Louis XIV of France standing in plate armour and blue sash facing left holding baton

[Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV), the "Sun King", was the [absolute monarch of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy_in_France) who made the country the [leading power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leading_power) in Europe.

During [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV)'s minority, trouble known as [The Fronde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fronde) occurred. This rebellion was driven by feudal lords and [sovereign courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament) as a reaction to the [royal absolute power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolutism_\(European_history\)). The monarchy reached its peak during the 17th century and reign of Louis XIV, during which France further increased its influence.[^cite_note-Joel_Colton-1978-62] By turning lords into [courtiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courtier) at the [Palace of Versailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Versailles), his command of the military went unchallenged. By the mid-17th century, France had emerged as the dominant power in Europe. Under the reign of Louis XIV, it built Europe's strongest army,[^cite_note-Lynn-63] fought a series of wars, and experienced remarkable artistic and scientific flourishing.[^cite_note-\:0-64] It also became the [most populous European country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_France) and had tremendous influence over European politics, economy, and culture. French became the most-used language in diplomacy, [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_science), and literature until the 20th century.[^cite_note-Language_and_Diplomacy-65] France took control of territories in the Americas, Africa and Asia. In 1685, Louis XIV [revoked the Edict of Nantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Fontainebleau), forcing thousands of Huguenots into exile and published the *[Code Noir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_Noir)* providing the legal framework for slavery and expelling Jews from French colonies.[^cite_note-66]&#x20;

Under the wars of [Louis XV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XV), France lost [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France) and most [Indian possessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_India) after its defeat in the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War) (1756–1763). [Its European territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_France) kept growing, however, with acquisitions such as [Lorraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorraine) and [Corsica](5714828.md). Louis XV's weak rule, including the decadence of his court, discredited the monarchy, which in part paved the way for the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution).[^cite_note-67]&#x20;

[Louis XVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XVI) [supported America with money, fleets and armies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War), helping them win [independence from Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War). France gained revenge but verged on bankruptcy—a factor that contributed to the Revolution. Some of the [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) occurred in French intellectual circles, and scientific breakthroughs, such as the [naming of oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_Lavoisier) and the first [hot air balloon carrying passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgolfier_brothers), were achieved by French scientists. French explorers took part in the [voyages of scientific exploration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_and_American_voyages_of_scientific_exploration) through maritime expeditions. Enlightenment philosophy, in which [reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationalism) is advocated as the primary source of [legitimacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_legitimacy), undermined the power of and support for the monarchy and was a factor in the Revolution.&#x20;

### Revolutionary France (1789–1799)

drawing of the Storming of the Bastille on 14 July 1789, smoke of gunfire enveloping stone castle

The [Storming of the Bastille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storming_of_the_Bastille) on 14 July 1789 was the most emblematic event of the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution).

The French Revolution was a period of political and societal change that began with the [Estates General of 1789](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estates_General_of_1789), and ended with the [coup of 18 Brumaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup_of_18_Brumaire) in 1799, leading to the formation of the [French Consulate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Consulate). Many of its ideas are fundamental principles of [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy),[^cite_note-68] while its values and institutions remain central to modern political discourse.[^cite_note-69]&#x20;

[Its causes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causes_of_the_French_Revolution) were a combination of social, political and economic factors, which the *[Ancien Régime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancien_R%C3%A9gime)* proved unable to manage. A financial crisis and social distress led in May 1789 to the [convocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convocation) of the [Estates General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estates_General_of_1789), which was converted into a [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(French_Revolution\)) in June. The [Storming of the Bastille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storming_of_the_Bastille) on 14 July led to a series of radical measures by the Assembly, among them the [abolition of feudalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolition_of_feudalism_in_France), state control over the [Catholic Church in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_France), and a [declaration of rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen).[^cite_note-70]&#x20;

The next three years were dominated by a struggle for political control, exacerbated by [economic depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_depression). Military defeats following the outbreak of the [French Revolutionary Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolutionary_Wars) in April 1792 resulted in the [insurrection of 10 August 1792](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurrection_of_10_August_1792). The [monarchy was abolished](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_the_abolition_of_the_monarchy) and replaced by the [French First Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_First_Republic) in September, while [Louis XVI was executed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_Louis_XVI) in January 1793.[^cite_note-71]&#x20;

After another [revolt in June 1793](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurrection_of_31_May_%E2%80%93_2_June_1793), the constitution was suspended and power passed from the [National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Convention) to the [Committee of Public Safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_Public_Safety). About 16,000 people were executed in a [Reign of Terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reign_of_Terror), which [ended in July 1794](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermidorian_Reaction). Weakened by external threats and internal opposition, the Republic was replaced in 1795 by the [Directory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Directory). In 1799, the [Consulate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Consulate) seized power in [a coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup_of_18_Brumaire) led by [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon).[^cite_note-72]&#x20;

### Napoleonic France (1799–1815)

Main article: [France in the long nineteenth century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_in_the_long_nineteenth_century)

painting of Napoleon in 1806 standing with hand in vest attended by staff and Imperial guard regiment

[Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon), [Emperor of the French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_of_the_French), built [a vast empire across Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_French_Empire).

[^cite_note-73]

[Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon) became [First Consul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Consulate) in 1799 and later [Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Year_XII) of the [French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_French_Empire). Changing sets of [European coalitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolutionary_and_Napoleonic_Wars) declared [wars on Napoleon's empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars). His armies conquered most of continental Europe with swift victories such as the [Austerlitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Austerlitz), [Jena-Auerstadt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Jena%E2%80%93Auerstedt) and [Battle of Friedland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Friedland). Members of the [Bonaparte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Bonaparte) family were appointed monarchs in some of the newly established kingdoms.[^cite_note-Blanning-74]&#x20;

These victories led to the worldwide expansion of French revolutionary ideals and reforms, such as the [metric system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metric_system), [Napoleonic Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Code) and [Declaration of the Rights of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen). In 1812 Napoleon [attacked Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_invasion_of_Russia), reaching [Moscow](19004.md). Thereafter his army disintegrated through supply problems, disease, Russian attacks, and finally winter. After this catastrophic campaign and the ensuing [uprising of European monarchies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Sixth_Coalition) against his rule, Napoleon was defeated. About 306,000 to a million Frenchmen [died during the Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars_casualties).[^cite_note-c-59][^cite_note-Blanning-74] After his [brief return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Days) from exile, Napoleon was finally defeated in 1815 at the [Battle of Waterloo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Waterloo), and the [Bourbon monarchy was restored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Restoration_in_France) with new constitutional limitations.&#x20;

### Colonial empire

The discredited Bourbon dynasty was overthrown by the [July Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_Revolution) of 1830, which established the constitutional [July Monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_Monarchy); French troops began the [conquest of Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_conquest_of_Algeria). Unrest led to the [French Revolution of 1848](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution_of_1848) and the end of the July Monarchy. The abolition of slavery and the introduction of male universal suffrage was re-enacted in 1848. In 1852, president of the French Republic, [Louis-Napoléon Bonaparte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III), Napoleon I's nephew, was proclaimed emperor of the [Second Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_French_Empire), as Napoleon III. He multiplied French interventions abroad, especially in [Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War), [Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_French_intervention_in_Mexico) and [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Italian_War_of_Independence). Napoleon III was unseated following defeat in the [Franco-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Prussian_War) of 1870, and his regime was replaced by the [Third Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Third_Republic). By 1875, the French conquest of Algeria was complete, with approximately 825,000 Algerians killed from famine, disease, and violence.[^cite_note-Kiernan2007-75] France suffered an estimated 10,000 killed and 35,000 wounded across all colonial campaigns.[^cite_note-c-59] A few thousand died in Mexico or [Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_conquest_of_Vietnam), but the vast majority perished in Algeria. Disease claimed an even heavier toll; one estimate puts total French and [Foreign Legion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Foreign_Legion) deaths from battle and disease for the entire 19th century at 110,000.[^cite_note-c-59]&#x20;



[French colonial empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonial_empire):&#x20;

&#x20; First

&#x20; Second

France had [colonial possessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonial_empire) since the beginning of the 17th century, but in the 19th and 20th centuries its empire extended significantly and became [the second-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_empires) behind the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire).[^cite_note-Lexington_Books-2005-76] Including metropolitan France, the total area reached almost 13 million square kilometres in the 1920s and 1930s, 9% of the world's land. Known as the *[Belle Époque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belle_%C3%89poque)*, the turn of the century was characterised by optimism, regional peace, economic prosperity and technological, scientific and cultural innovations. In 1905, [state secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) was [officially established](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1905_French_law_on_the_Separation_of_the_Churches_and_the_State).[^cite_note-77]&#x20;

### Early to mid-20th century (1914–1946)

Main article: [History of France (1900–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_France_\(1900%E2%80%93present\))



French [Poilus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poilu) posing with their war-torn flag in 1917, during World War I

During World War I, France played a [decisive role](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Army_in_World_War_I) in defeating the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) as part of the [Triple Entente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_Entente), while participating in the majority of the war's major battles fought on the Western Front.[^cite_note-78] A rich industrial area in the north was occupied. France and the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) emerged victorious against the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) at tremendous human cost. It left 1.4 million French soldiers dead, 4% of its population.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80] In addition, France suffered 4,266,000 wounded.[^cite_note-c-59] Interwar was marked by [intense international tensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Events_preceding_World_War_II_in_Europe) and social reforms introduced by the [Popular Front government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front_\(France\)) (e.g., [annual leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_leave), [eight-hour workdays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eight-hour_day), [women in government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_government)).&#x20;

In 1940, France was [invaded and quickly defeated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_France) by [Nazi Germany](21212.md). France was divided into a [German occupation zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_military_administration_in_occupied_France_during_World_War_II) in the north, an [Italian occupation zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_occupation_of_France) and an unoccupied territory, the rest of France, which consisted of southern France and the French empire. The [Vichy government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vichy_France), an authoritarian regime collaborating with Germany, ruled the unoccupied territory. [Free France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_France), the government-in-exile led by [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle), was set up in London.[^cite_note-81]&#x20;

From 1942 to 1944, about 160,000 French citizens, including around [75,000 Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust_in_France),[^cite_note-82] were deported to [death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extermination_camp) and [concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment).[^cite_note-83] On 6 June 1944, the Allies [invaded Normandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Overlord), and in August they [invaded Provence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Dragoon). The Allies and [French Resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Resistance) emerged victorious, and French sovereignty was restored with the [Provisional Government of the French Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_the_French_Republic) (GPRF). This interim government, established by de Gaulle, continued to [wage war against Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Allied_invasion_of_Germany) and to [purge collaborators from office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89puration_l%C3%A9gale). It made important reforms e.g. suffrage extended to women and the creation of a [social security](16980827.md) system.&#x20;

### 1946–present

Charles de Gaulle seated in uniform looking left with folded arms

[Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle), a hero of World War I, leader of the [Free French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_French_Forces) during [World War II](32927.md), and [President of France](24899.md)

A new constitution resulted in the [Fourth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Fourth_Republic) (1946–1958), which saw strong economic growth (*les [Trente Glorieuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trente_Glorieuses)*). France was a founding member of [NATO](21133.md) and attempted to [regain control of French Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Indochina_War), but was defeated by the [Viet Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Minh) in 1954. France faced another [anti-colonialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-colonialist) [conflict in Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_War), then part of France and home to over one million European settlers ([Pied-Noir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pied-Noir)). The French systematically used torture and repression, including extrajudicial killings to keep control.[^cite_note-Macqueen2014-84] This conflict nearly led to a coup and civil war.[^cite_note-85]&#x20;

During the [May 1958 crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_1958_crisis_in_France), the weak Fourth Republic gave way to the [Fifth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Fifth_Republic), which included a strengthened presidency.[^cite_note-86] The war concluded with the [Évian Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89vian_Accords) in 1962 which led to [Algerian independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_Algerian_independence_referendum), at a high price: between half a million and one million deaths and over 2 million internally-displaced Algerians.[^cite_note-Springer-87] Around one million Pied-Noirs and [Harkis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harki) fled from Algeria to France.[^cite_note-google4-88] A vestige of the empire is the [French overseas departments and territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_France).&#x20;

During the [Cold War](325329.md), de Gaulle pursued a policy of "national independence" towards the [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc) and [Eastern blocs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc). He withdrew from NATO's military-integrated command (while remaining within the alliance), launched a [nuclear development programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Force_de_dissuasion) and made France the [fourth nuclear power](589091.md). He [restored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89lys%C3%A9e_Treaty) cordial [Franco-German relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France%E2%80%93Germany_relations) to create a European counterweight between American and Soviet spheres of influence. However, he opposed any development of a [supranational Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supranational_union), favouring [sovereign nations](23604120.md). The revolt of [May 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_68) had an enormous social impact; it was a watershed moment when a conservative moral ideal (religion, patriotism, respect for authority) shifted to a more liberal moral ideal (secularism, individualism, sexual revolution). Although the revolt was a political failure (the [Gaullist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaullism) party emerged stronger than before) it announced a split between the French and de Gaulle, who resigned.[^cite_note-89]&#x20;

In the post-Gaullist era, France remained one of the most developed [economies in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) but faced crises that resulted in high unemployment rates and increasing public debt. In the late 20th and early 21st centuries, France has been at the forefront of the development of a supranational [European Union](9317.md), notably by signing the [Maastricht Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maastricht_Treaty) in 1992, establishing the [eurozone](184391.md) in 1999[^cite_note-superficy-90] and signing the [Treaty of Lisbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lisbon) in 2007.[^cite_note-91] France has fully reintegrated into NATO and since participated in most NATO-sponsored wars.[^cite_note-92]&#x20;



[Calais Jungle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calais_Jungle)

Since the 19th century, France has [received many immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_France), often male [foreign workers](384142.md) from European Catholic countries who generally returned home when not employed.[^cite_note-Marie-Christine_Weidmann-Koop-93] During the 1970s, France faced an economic crisis and allowed new immigrants (mostly from the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb), in northwest Africa)[^cite_note-Marie-Christine_Weidmann-Koop-93] to permanently [settle in France with their families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_reunification) and acquire citizenship. It resulted in hundreds of thousands of Muslims living in subsidised public housing and suffering from high unemployment rates.[^cite_note-94] The government had a policy of [assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation) of immigrants, where they were expected to adhere to French values and norms.[^cite_note-95] From the 2000s, France experienced continued immigration from Africa, the Middle East, and Asia. In addition, [Calais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migrants_around_Calais) became a transit area for migrants.&#x20;

Since the [1995 public transport bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_France_bombings), France has been targeted by Islamist organisations, notably the [*Charlie Hebdo* attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2015_%C3%8Ele-de-France_attacks) in 2015, which provoked the [largest public rallies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_marches) in French history, gathering 4.4 million people, and[^cite_note-96] the [November 2015 Paris attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2015_Paris_attacks), which resulted in 130 deaths, the deadliest attack on French soil since World War II[^cite_note-97] and the deadliest in the European Union since the [Madrid train bombings in 2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Madrid_train_bombings).[^cite_note-98] [Opération Chammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op%C3%A9ration_Chammal), France's military efforts to contain [ISIS](9087364.md), killed over 1,000 [ISIS](9087364.md) troops between 2014 and 2015.[^cite_note-99]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_France)



[Chamonix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamonix) valley with [Mont Blanc](44768.md) in the background, the highest mountain in the [Alps](981.md) and [Western Europe](33800.md) on the border with [Italy](14532.md)

The vast majority of France's territory and population is situated in Western Europe and is called [Metropolitan France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_France). It is bordered by the [North Sea](21179.md) to the north, the [English Channel](9230.md) to the northwest, the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) to the west, and the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to the southeast. Its land borders consist of [Belgium](3343.md) and [Luxembourg](17515.md) in the northeast, [Germany](11867.md) and [Switzerland](26748.md) in the east, [Italy](14532.md) and [Monaco](19261.md) in the southeast, and [Andorra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andorra) and [Spain](26667.md) in the south and southwest. Except for the northeast, most of France's land borders are roughly delineated by natural boundaries and geographic features: to the south, the [Pyrenees](24707.md) and the [Alps](981.md); to the southeast, the [Jura Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jura_Mountains); and to the east, the [Rhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine) river. Including [Overseas France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_France), it also borders [Suriname](26828.md) and [Brazil](3383.md) through [French Guiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Guiana) in [South America](26769.md), and the [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_the_Netherlands) through [Saint Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Martin_\(island\)) in the [Caribbean](18956035.md). Metropolitan France includes various coastal islands, of which the largest is [Corsica](5714828.md). Metropolitan France is situated mostly between latitudes [41°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/41st_parallel_north) and [51° N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/51st_parallel_north), and longitudes [6° W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_meridian_west) and [10° E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10th_meridian_east), on the western edge of Europe, and thus lies within the northern [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climate) zone. Its continental part covers about 1,000 km from north to south and from east to west.&#x20;

Metropolitan France covers 551,500 square kilometres (212,935 sq mi),[^cite_note-France-100] the largest among [European Union](9317.md) members.[^cite_note-superficy-90] France's total land area, with its overseas departments and territories (excluding [Adélie Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad%C3%A9lie_Land)), is 643,801 km<sup>2</sup> (248,573 sq mi),[^cite_note-Field_Listing_::_Area-101] 0.45% of the total land area on Earth. France possesses a wide variety of landscapes, from coastal plains in the north and west to the mountain ranges of the Alps in the southeast, the [Massif Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massif_Central) in the south-central and [Pyrenees](24707.md) in the southwest. With numerous [overseas departments and territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_France) scattered across the planet, France possesses the second-largest [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone) (EEZ) in the world, covering 11,035,000 km<sup>2</sup> (4,261,000 sq mi). Its EEZ covers approximately 8% of the total surface of all the EEZs of the world.&#x20;

### Geology, topography and hydrography



Geological formations near [Roussillon, Vaucluse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roussillon,_Vaucluse)

Metropolitan France has a wide variety of topographic sets and natural landscapes. During the [Hercynian uplift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hercynian_uplift) in the [Paleozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleozoic) Era, the [Armorican Massif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armorican_Massif), the Massif Central, the [Morvan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morvan), the [Vosges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vosges) and [Ardennes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ardennes) ranges and the island of Corsica were formed. These massifs delineate several sedimentary basins such as the [Aquitaine Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquitaine_Basin) in the southwest and the [Paris Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Basin) in the north. Various routes of natural passage, such as the [Rhône Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rh%C3%B4ne_Valley), allow easy communication. The Alpine, Pyrenean and Jura Mountains are much younger and have less eroded forms. At 4,810.45 metres (15,782 ft)[^cite_note-102] above sea level, [Mont Blanc](44768.md) in the Alps is the highest point in Western Europe. 60% of municipalities are classified as having moderate seismic risk.&#x20;

The coastlines offer contrasting landscapes: mountain ranges along the [French Riviera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Riviera), coastal cliffs such as the [Côte d'Albâtre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B4te_d%27Alb%C3%A2tre), and wide sandy plains in the [Languedoc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languedoc). Corsica lies off the Mediterranean coast. France has an extensive river system consisting of the four major rivers: the [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine), the [Loire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loire), the [Garonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garonne), the [Rhône](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rh%C3%B4ne) and their tributaries, whose combined catchment includes over 62% of the metropolitan territory. The Rhône divides the Massif Central from the Alps and flows into the Mediterranean Sea at the [Camargue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camargue). The Garonne meets the [Dordogne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dordogne_\(river\)) just after [Bordeaux](4097.md), forming the [Gironde estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gironde_estuary), the largest estuary in Western Europe which after approximately 100 kilometres (62 mi) empties into the Atlantic Ocean.[^cite_note-103] Other water courses drain towards the [Meuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meuse) and Rhine along the northeastern borders. France has 11,000,000 km<sup>2</sup> (4,200,000 sq mi) of marine waters within three oceans under its jurisdiction, of which 97% are overseas.&#x20;

### Environment

See also: [List of national parks of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_France), [Regional natural parks of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_natural_parks_of_France), and [Climate change in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_France)

On 28 June 2019, in [Vérargues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%A9rargues), the temperature reached 46 °C (115 °F), setting a temperature record in Metropolitan France since records began.[^cite_note-104] [Climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_France) means that this kind of heatwave in France can be expected to occur 10 times more frequently than it would have done a century ago.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] France was one of the first countries to create an environment ministry, in 1971.[^cite_note-107] France is ranked [19th by carbon dioxide emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_carbon_dioxide_emissions) due to the country's heavy investment in [nuclear power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_France) following the [1973 oil crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis),[^cite_note-108] which now accounts for 75 per cent of its electricity production[^cite_note-109] and results in less pollution.[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111] According to the 2020 [Environmental Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_Performance_Index) conducted by [Yale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale) and [Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University), France was the fifth most environmentally conscious country in the world.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] Like all European Union member states, France agreed to cut carbon emissions by at least 20% of 1990 levels by 2020.[^cite_note-114] As of 2009, carbon dioxide emissions per capita are lower than that of China.[^cite_note-115] The country was set to impose a [carbon tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_tax) in 2009;[^cite_note-116] however, the plan was abandoned due to fears of it burdening French businesses.[^cite_note-117]&#x20;



The [Gironde estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gironde_estuary) is classified as a [marine nature park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gironde_estuary_and_Pertuis_sea_Marine_Nature_Park) and is part of the [Natura 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natura_2000) network. The left bank is part of the [Médoc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9doc) [regional nature park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_nature_parks_of_France). With a surface area of 635 km

2

&#x20;(245 sq mi), it is the largest estuary in Europe.

Forests account for 31 per cent of the land area—the fourth-highest proportion in Europe—representing an increase of 7 per cent since 1990.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] The forests are some of the most diverse in Europe, comprising more than 140 species of trees.[^cite_note-121] France had a 2018 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 4.52/10, ranking it 123rd globally.[^cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-122] There are nine [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_park)[^cite_note-123] and 46 [natural parks](100406.md).[^cite_note-124] A regional nature park[^cite_note-125] (French: *parc naturel régional* or PNR) is a public establishment in France between local authorities and the national government covering an inhabited rural area of outstanding beauty, to protect the scenery and heritage as well as setting up sustainable economic development in the area.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-FGTO-127] As of 2019, there are 54 PNRs in France.[^cite_note-128]&#x20;

## Politics

Main article: [Politics of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_France)

France is a [representative democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representative_democracy) organised as a unitary [semi-presidential republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_republic).[^cite_note-129] Democratic traditions and values are deeply rooted in French culture, identity and politics.[^cite_note-EncyclopediaBritannica-130] The [Constitution of the Fifth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_France) was approved by [referendum in 1958](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1958_French_constitutional_referendum), establishing a framework consisting of executive, legislative and judicial branches.[^cite_note-131] It sought to address the instability of the Third and Fourth Republics by combining elements of both the parliamentary and presidential systems, while greatly strengthening the authority of the executive relative to the legislature.[^cite_note-EncyclopediaBritannica-130]&#x20;

### Government

Main article: [Government of France](3398091.md)



[Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md)\
[President](24899.md)



[Sébastien Lecornu](54364868.md)\
[Prime Minister](218751.md)

&#x20;The executive branch has two leaders. The [president](24899.md), who is [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) since 2017, is the [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state), elected directly by [universal adult suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage) for a five-year term.[^cite_note-132] The [prime minister](218751.md), who is [Sébastien Lecornu](54364868.md) since 2025, is the [head of government](37570.md), appointed by the president to lead the government. The president has the power to dissolve [parliament](1009776.md) or circumvent it by submitting referendums directly to the people; the president also appoints judges and civil servants, negotiates and ratifies international agreements, and serves as [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the [French Armed Forces](10724.md). The prime minister determines public policy and oversees the civil service, with an emphasis on domestic matters.[^cite_note-133] In the [2022 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_French_presidential_election), Macron was re-elected.[^cite_note-134] Two months later in the [legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_French_legislative_election), Macron lost his parliamentary majority and had to form a [minority government](271434.md).[^cite_note-135]



[Palais Bourbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_Bourbon) is the meeting place of the [National Assembly](299616.md), the lower legislative chamber of the [French Parliament](1009776.md).

The legislature consists of the French Parliament, a [bicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameralism) body made up of a [lower house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_house), the [National Assembly](299616.md), and an [upper house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_house), the [Senate](567323.md).[^cite_note-136] Legislators in the National Assembly, known as *[députés](29334549.md)*, represent local constituencies and are [directly elected](652563.md) for five-year terms.[^cite_note-137] The Assembly has the power to dismiss the government by majority vote. Senators are chosen by an [electoral college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_college) for six-year terms, with half the seats submitted to election every three years.[^cite_note-138] The Senate's legislative powers are limited; in the event of disagreement between the two chambers, the National Assembly has the final say.[^cite_note-139] The parliament is responsible for determining the rules and principles concerning most areas of law, political amnesty, and fiscal policy; however, the government may draft specific details concerning most laws.&#x20;

From World War II until 2017, French politics was dominated by two politically opposed groupings: one left-wing, the [French Section of the Workers' International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Section_of_the_Workers%27_International), which was succeeded by the [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_\(France\)) in 1969; and the other right-wing, the [Gaullist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaullism), whose name changed over time to the [Rally of the French People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rally_of_the_French_People) (1947), the [Union of Democrats for the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Democrats_for_the_Republic) (1958), the [Rally for the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rally_for_the_Republic) (1976), the [Union for a Popular Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_for_a_Popular_Movement) (2007) and [The Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_\(France\)) (since 2015). In the [2017 presidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_presidential_election) and [legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_legislative_election), the [radical centrist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_centrism) party [La République En Marche!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_R%C3%A9publique_En_Marche!) (LREM) became the dominant force, overtaking both Socialists and Republicans. LREM's opponent in the second round of the 2017 and 2022 presidential elections was the growing far-right party [National Rally](67119.md) (RN). Since 2020, [The Ecologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ecologists) have performed well in mayoral elections in major cities[^cite_note-140] while on a national level, the [New Ecological and Social People's Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Ecological_and_Social_People%27s_Union) was the second-largest voting block elected to the lower house in 2022.[^cite_note-141] [Right-wing populist](8354382.md) RN became the largest opposition party in the National Assembly in 2022.[^cite_note-142]&#x20;

The electorate is constitutionally empowered to vote on amendments passed by the Parliament and bills submitted by the president. Referendums have played a key role in shaping French politics and even foreign policy; voters have decided on such matters as Algeria's independence, the election of the president by popular vote, the formation of the EU, and the reduction of presidential term limits.[^cite_note-143]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_France)

France is divided into 18 [regions](58846.md) (located in Europe and overseas), five [overseas collectivities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_collectivity) ([French Polynesia](10737.md), [Saint Pierre and Miquelon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Pierre_and_Miquelon), [Wallis and Futuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallis_and_Futuna), [Saint Barthélemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Barth%C3%A9lemy), and [Saint Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivity_of_Saint_Martin)), one [overseas territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_territory_\(France\)) ([French Southern and Antarctic Lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Southern_and_Antarctic_Lands)), one special collectivity ([New Caledonia](21342.md)), and one uninhabited island ([Clipperton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clipperton_Island)) under the authority of the [Minister of Overseas France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_the_Overseas).&#x20;
