### Ethnic groups

Historically, [French people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_people) were mainly of [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_peoples)-[Gallic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauls) origin, with a significant admixture of [Italic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italic_peoples) ([Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire)) and [Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples) ([Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franks)) groups reflecting centuries of respective migration and settlement.[^cite_note-262] Through the course of the Middle Ages, France incorporated various neighbouring ethnic and linguistic groups, as evidenced by [Breton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bretons) elements in the west, [Aquitanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquitani) in the southwest, [Scandinavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavia) in the northwest, [Alemannic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alemanni) in the northeast, and [Ligurian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ligures) in the southeast.&#x20;

Large-scale immigration over the last century and a half have led to a more multicultural society; beginning with the French Revolution and further codified in the French Constitution of 1958, the government is prohibited from collecting data on ethnicity and ancestry; most demographic information is drawn from private sector organisations or academic institutions. In 2004 the *Institut Montaigne* estimated that within Metropolitan France, 51 million people were white (85% of the population), 6 million were northwest African (10%), 2 million were black (3.3%), and 1 million were Asian (1.7%).[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]&#x20;

A 2008 poll conducted jointly by the [Institut national d'études démographiques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institut_national_d%27%C3%A9tudes_d%C3%A9mographiques) (INSEE) and [the French National Institute of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institut_national_de_la_statistique_et_des_%C3%A9tudes_%C3%A9conomiques)[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-Oppenheimer-266] estimated that the largest minority ancestry groups were [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italians_in_France) (5 million), followed by [northwest African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb) (3–6 million),[^cite_note-Cohen1995-267][^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269] [Sub-Saharan African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_French_people) (2.5 million), Armenian (500,000), and Turkish (200,000).[^cite_note-270] There are also sizeable minorities of other [European ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Europe), namely [Spanish](963152.md), [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people), [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_people), and [Greek](42056.md).[^cite_note-Cohen1995-267][^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] France has a significant [Gitan (Romani)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people_in_France) population, numbering between 20,000 and 400,000;[^cite_note-273] many foreign [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) are [expelled back to Bulgaria and Romania frequently](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_Roma_migrants_from_France).[^cite_note-274]&#x20;

### Immigration

Main article: [Immigration to France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_France)

It is estimated that 40% of the French population is descended at least partially from the different waves of immigration since the early 20th century;[^cite_note-275] between 1921 and 1935 alone, about 1.1 million net immigrants came to France.[^cite_note-276] The next largest wave came in the 1960s when around 1.6 million *[pieds-noirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pieds-noirs)* returned to France following the independence of its northwest African possessions of Algeria and Morocco.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278] They were joined by numerous former colonial subjects from North and West Africa, as well as numerous European immigrants from Spain and Portugal.&#x20;



The [Calais Jungle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calais_Jungle) was a [refugee](45547.md) and [illegal migrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_migrant) encampment in the vicinity of [Calais](85468.md), France, that existed from January 2015 to October 2016.

France remains a major destination for immigrants, accepting about 200,000 legal immigrants annually.[^cite_note-279] In 2005, it was Western Europe's leading recipient of asylum seekers, with an estimated 50,000 applications (albeit a 15% decrease from 2004).[^cite_note-UNHCR-280] In 2010, France received about 48,100 asylum applications—placing it among the top five asylum recipients in the world.[^cite_note-281] In subsequent years it saw the number of applications increase, ultimately doubling to 100,412 in 2017.[^cite_note-282] The European Union allows free movement between the member states, although France established controls to curb Eastern European migration. Foreigners' rights are established in the [Code of Entry and Residence of Foreigners and of the Right to Asylum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_of_Entry_and_Residence_of_Foreigners_and_of_the_Right_to_Asylum). Immigration remains a contentious political issue.[^cite_note-283]&#x20;

In 2008 INSEE estimated that the number of foreign-born immigrants was around 5 million (8% of the population), while their French-born descendants numbered 6.5 million, or 11% of the population. Thus, nearly a fifth of the country's population were either first or second-generation immigrants, of which more than 5 million were of European origin and 4 million of [Maghrebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghrebis) ancestry.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-Insee_1-285][^cite_note-INSEE1-286] In 2008, France [granted citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_nationality_law) to 137,000 persons, mostly from Morocco, Algeria and Turkey.[^cite_note-287] In 2022, more than 320,000 migrants came to France, with the majority coming from [Africa](5334607.md).[^cite_note-288]&#x20;

In 2014, the INSEE reported a significant increase in the number of immigrants coming from Spain, Portugal and Italy between 2009 and 2012. According to the institute, this increase resulted from the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis).[^cite_note-sudouest.fr-289] Statistics on Spanish immigrants in France show a growth of 107 per cent between 2009 and 2012, with the population growing from 5,300 to 11,000.[^cite_note-sudouest.fr-289] Of the total of 229,000 foreigners coming to France in 2012, nearly 8% were Portuguese, 5% British, 5% Spanish, 4% Italian, 4% German, 3% Romanian, and 3% Belgian.[^cite_note-sudouest.fr-289]&#x20;

### Language

Main articles: [French language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language), [Languages of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_France), and [Organisation internationale de la Francophonie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie)

world map of French speaking countries

Map of the [Francophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_territorial_entities_where_French_is_an_official_language) world:&#x20;

&#x20; Native language

&#x20; Administrative language

&#x20; Secondary or cultural language

The official language is French,[^cite_note-290] a [Romance language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_language) derived from [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin). Since 1635, the [Académie Française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acad%C3%A9mie_Fran%C3%A7aise) has been the official authority on the French language, although its recommendations carry no legal weight. There are also regional languages spoken in France, such as [Occitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occitan_language), [Breton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breton_language), [Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language), [Flemish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Flemish) ([Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language) dialect), [Alsatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alsatian_dialect) (German dialect), [Basque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_language), and [Corsican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corsican_language) (Italian dialect). Italian was the official language of Corsica up to 1859.[^cite_note-291] Although regional languages do not have the status of official languages, they are recognised by Article 75-1 of the constitution as part of France's heritage.[^cite_note-292]&#x20;

The government does not regulate the choice of language in publications by individuals, but the use of French is required by law in commercial and workplace communications. The French government tries to promote French in the EU and globally through institutions such as the [Organisation internationale de la Francophonie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie). There are 77 vernacular minority languages, eight spoken in Metropolitan France and 69 in the overseas territories.&#x20;

According to the 2007 Adult Education Survey, part of a project by the European Union and carried out in France by INSEE and based on a sample of 15,350 persons, French was the native language of 87% of the population, or roughly 56 million people, followed by Arabic (3.6%, 2.3 million), Portuguese (1.5%, 960,000), Spanish (1.2%, 770,000) and Italian (1.0%, 640,000).[^cite_note-293]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_France)

Notre-Dame de Reims façade, gothic stone cathedral against blue sky

[Notre-Dame de Reims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reims_Cathedral) is the Catholic cathedral where the [Kings of France were crowned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_the_French_monarch) until 1825.

[^cite_note-294]

France is a secular country in which [freedom of religion](46006.md) is a constitutional right. After alternating between royal and secular republican governments during the 19th century, the [1905 law on the Separation of the Churches and the State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1905_French_law_on_the_Separation_of_the_Churches_and_the_State) established the concept of *[laïcité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism_in_France)*, a strict [separation of church and state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_church_and_state) under which the government and public life are kept completely secular, detached from any religion.[^cite_note-georgetown2-295] [Alsace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alsace) and [Moselle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moselle_\(department\)), which were part of the [German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) when state secularism was established in France, is an exception since the [local law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concordat_in_Alsace%E2%80%93Moselle) stipulates official status and state funding for [Lutheranism](23371382.md), [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism), and [Judaism](15624.md).[^cite_note-religion2020-296]&#x20;

[Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_France) has been the main religion in France for more than a millennium, and it was once the [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion).[^cite_note-297] Its role nowadays has been greatly reduced; nevertheless in 2012, among the 47,000 religious buildings in France, 94% were Catholic churches.[^cite_note-298] The government is prohibited from recognising specific rights to any religious community (with the exception of legacy statutes like those of military chaplains and the aforementioned local law in Alsace-Moselle). It recognises religious organisations according to formal legal criteria that do not address religious doctrine, and religious organisations are expected to refrain from intervening in policymaking.[^cite_note-299] Some religious groups, such as [scientology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientology), the [Children of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Family_International), the [Unification Church](512933.md), and the [Order of the Solar Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Solar_Temple), are considered [cults](1008588.md) (*sectes* in French) and are not granted the same status as recognised religions.[^cite_note-300]&#x20;

### Health

Pitié-Salpêtrière Hospital in Paris, stone building with slate dome

The [Pitié-Salpêtrière Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piti%C3%A9-Salp%C3%AAtri%C3%A8re_Hospital), a teaching hospital in Paris, is one of Europe's largest hospitals.

[^cite_note-301]

The [French health care system](20603142.md) is one of [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) largely financed by government [national health insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_health_insurance). In its 2000 assessment of world health care systems, the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) found that France provided the "close to best overall health care" in the world.[^cite_note-who.int-302] The French health care system was ranked first worldwide by the WHO in 1997.[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304] In 2011, France spent 11.6% of its GDP on health care, or US\$4,086 per capita,[^cite_note-WHO_country_facts:_France-305] a figure much higher than the average spent by countries in Europe. Approximately 77% of health expenditures are covered by government-funded agencies.[^cite_note-306]&#x20;

Care is generally free for people affected by chronic diseases such as cancer, AIDS or cystic fibrosis. The [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) at birth is 80 years for men and 85.6 years for women.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308] There are 3.2 physicians for every 1000 inhabitants, 5.7 hospital beds for every 1000 inhabitants, and average health care spending per capita was US\$8,630 in 2022, representing 12.1% of GDP.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310] As of 2007, approximately 140,000 inhabitants (0.4%) are living with HIV/AIDS.[^cite_note-France-100]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in France](379033.md)



The [ENS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_normale_sup%C3%A9rieure_\(Paris\)) produces among the most [Nobel Prize](21201.md) laureates [per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita) in the world.

[^cite_note-311]

In 1802, Napoleon created the [lycée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education_in_France), the second and final stage of secondary education that prepares students for higher education studies or a profession.[^cite_note-312] [Jules Ferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Ferry) is considered the father of the French modern school, leading reforms in the late 19th century that established free, secular and compulsory education (currently mandatory to age 16).[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314]&#x20;

French education is centralised and divided into three stages: primary, secondary, and higher education. The [Programme for International Student Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment), coordinated by the OECD, ranked France's education as near the OECD average in 2018.[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-oecd-316] School children in France reported greater concern about the disciplinary climate and behaviour in classrooms compared to other OECD countries.[^cite_note-oecd-316]&#x20;

Higher education is divided between [public universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_and_colleges_in_France) and the prestigious and selective *[Grandes écoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grande_%C3%A9cole)*, such as [Sciences Po Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sciences_Po) for political studies, [HEC Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HEC_Paris) for economics, [Polytechnique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_Polytechnique), the [École des hautes études en sciences sociales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_des_hautes_%C3%A9tudes_en_sciences_sociales) for social studies and the [École nationale supérieure des mines de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_nationale_sup%C3%A9rieure_des_mines_de_Paris) that produce high-profile engineers, or the [École nationale d'administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_nationale_d%27administration) for careers in the [Grands Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grands_corps_de_l%27%C3%89tat) of the state. The *Grandes écoles* have been criticised for alleged elitism, producing many if not most of France's high-ranking civil servants, CEOs and politicians.[^cite_note-gécoles-317]&#x20;

France also supports a network of schools internationally through the [Agency for French Education Abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agency_for_French_Education_Abroad). The network includes a mix of directly managed schools, subsidized institutions, and partner schools, and provides French education to students around the world.[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_France)

### Art

Further information: [French art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_art) and [Art in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_in_Paris)



[Claude Monet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Monet), founder of the [Impressionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionism) movement

The origins of a distinctly [French art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_art) date back to the Middle Ages. In the 12th century, Paris and its region saw the birth of [Gothic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_art), known at the time as *Opus Francigenum* (lit. 'French work'), which from the cathedrals of [Chartres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chartres_Cathedral), [Amiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amiens_Cathedral) and [Reims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reims_Cathedral) soon radiated throughout Europe, thanks to French architects and sculptors who went abroad.[^cite_note-320]&#x20;

French art was very much influenced first by [Flemish art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flemish_painting) then by [Italian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_art) at the time of the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance). [Jean Fouquet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Fouquet), the most famous medieval [French painter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_French_painters), is said to have been the first to travel to Italy and experience the Early Renaissance firsthand. The Renaissance painting [School of Fontainebleau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Fontainebleau) was directly inspired by Italian painters such as [Primaticcio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primaticcio) and [Rosso Fiorentino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosso_Fiorentino), who both worked in France. Two of the most famous French artists of the time of the [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque) era, [Nicolas Poussin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Poussin) and [Claude Lorrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Lorrain), lived in Italy. But [17th-century French art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/17th-century_French_art) quickly moved towards classicism under [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV), with artists like painter [Charles Le Brun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Le_Brun), architect [Louis Le Vau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Le_Vau), garden and landscape architect [André Le Nôtre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Le_N%C3%B4tre) and cabinetmaker [André-Charles Boulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9-Charles_Boulle) who all worked at the construction of the [Palace of Versailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Versailles) establishing the fashionable [Louis XIV style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV_style) at a time when France was becoming a cultural reference point for all of Europe.[^cite_note-321]&#x20;

French artists developed the [rococo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rococo) style in the early 18th century, as a more intimate evolution of the Baroque style, the works of the court-endorsed artists [Antoine Watteau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_Watteau), [François Boucher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Boucher) and [Jean-Honoré Fragonard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Honor%C3%A9_Fragonard) being the most representative in the country. The French Revolution brought great changes, as Napoleon favoured artists of [neoclassic style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassicism) such as [Jacques-Louis David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques-Louis_David) and the highly influential [Académie des Beaux-Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acad%C3%A9mie_des_Beaux-Arts) defined the style known as [Academism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_art).&#x20;

In the second part of the 19th century, France's influence over painting grew, with the development of new styles of painting such as [Impressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionism) and [Symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolist_painting). The most famous impressionist painters of the period were [Camille Pissarro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camille_Pissarro), [Édouard Manet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89douard_Manet), [Edgar Degas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Degas), [Claude Monet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Monet) and [Auguste Renoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_Renoir).[^cite_note-322] The second generation of impressionist-style painters, [Paul Cézanne](24472.md), [Paul Gauguin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Gauguin), [Toulouse-Lautrec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toulouse-Lautrec) and [Georges Seurat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Seurat), were also at the avant-garde of artistic evolutions,[^cite_note-323] as well as the [fauvist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauvism) artists [Henri Matisse](60203.md), [André Derain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Derain) and [Maurice de Vlaminck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_de_Vlaminck).[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325]&#x20;

At the beginning of the 20th century, [Cubism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubism) was developed by [Georges Braque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Braque) and the Spanish painter [Pablo Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Picasso), who was living in Paris.[^cite_note-326] Other foreign artists also settled and worked in or near Paris, such as [Vincent van Gogh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincent_van_Gogh) and [Wassily Kandinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wassily_Kandinsky).[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328] In the early 20th century and in particular during the interwar period, a loose collection of foreign artists of whom many were Jewish formed the [School of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Paris). Amongst these artists were [Marc Chagall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Chagall), [Amedeo Modigliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amedeo_Modigliani), [Yitzhak Frenel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandre_Frenel), [Jules Pascin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Pascin) and others.[^cite_note-Nieszawer-2020-329] The Second World War led to the deaths or exile of multiple artists, resulting in the relative fall of Paris' domination and centrality in the world art scene in favour of New York City.[^cite_note-330]&#x20;

### Architecture

Main article: [French architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_architecture)

Sainte Chapelle interior showing painted stonework vaulting and stained glass

[Saint Louis'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_IX_of_France) [Sainte-Chapelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sainte-Chapelle) represents the French impact on religious architecture.

During the Middle Ages, many fortified castles were built by feudal nobles to mark their powers. Some French castles that survive are [Chinon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinon_\(castle\)), [Château d'Angers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_d%27Angers), [Château de Vincennes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_de_Vincennes) and the [Cathar castles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathar_castles). During this era, France had been using [Romanesque architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanesque_architecture) like most of Western Europe.&#x20;

[Gothic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture), originally named *Opus Francigenum* meaning "French work",[^cite_note-331] was born in [Île-de-France](1716427.md) and was the first French style of architecture to be imitated throughout Europe.[^cite_note-332] Northern France is the home of some of the most important Gothic cathedrals and basilicas, the first of these being the [Saint Denis Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Saint-Denis) (used as the royal necropolis); other important French Gothic cathedrals are [Notre-Dame de Chartres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chartres_Cathedral) and [Notre-Dame d'Amiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amiens_Cathedral). The kings were crowned in another important Gothic church: [Notre-Dame de Reims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reims_Cathedral).[^cite_note-333]&#x20;

The final victory in the Hundred Years' War marked an important stage in the evolution of French architecture. It was the time of the [French Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Renaissance) and several artists from Italy were invited to the French court; many residential palaces were built in the [Loire Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loire_Valley), from 1450 as a first reference the [Château de Montsoreau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_de_Montsoreau).[^cite_note-334] Examples of such residential castles include the [Château de Chambord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_de_Chambord), the [Château de Chenonceau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_de_Chenonceau), or the [Château d'Amboise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_d%27Amboise).&#x20;

Following the Renaissance and the end of the Middle Ages, [Baroque architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Baroque_architecture) replaced the traditional Gothic style. Baroque architecture found greater success in the secular domain than in the religious one.[^cite_note-335] In the secular domain, the [Palace of Versailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Versailles) has many Baroque features. [Jules Hardouin-Mansart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Hardouin-Mansart), who designed the extensions to Versailles, was one of the most influential French architects of the Baroque era; he is famous for his dome at [Les Invalides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Invalides).[^cite_note-336] Impressive provincial Baroque architecture is found in places that were not yet French, such as [Place Stanislas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Stanislas) in [Nancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy,_France). On the military architectural side, [Vauban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9bastien_Le_Prestre,_Marquis_of_Vauban) designed some of the most efficient fortresses in Europe and became an influential military architect; as a result, imitations of his works can be found all over Europe, the Americas, Russia and Turkey.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338]&#x20;

After the Revolution, the [Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism) favoured [Neoclassicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassicism) although it was introduced in France before the revolution with such buildings as the [Parisian Pantheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panth%C3%A9on) or the [Capitole de Toulouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitole_de_Toulouse). Built during the French First Empire, the [Arc de Triomphe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_de_Triomphe) and [Sainte Marie-Madeleine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89glise_de_la_Madeleine) represent the best example of [Empire style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_style) architecture.[^cite_note-339] Under [Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III), a wave of urbanism and architecture was given birth; extravagant buildings such as the neo-Baroque [Palais Garnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_Garnier) were built. The urban planning of the time was very organised and rigorous; most notably, [Haussmann's renovation of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haussmann%27s_renovation_of_Paris). The architecture associated with this era is named [Second Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Empire_\(architecture\)) in English, the term being taken from the [Second French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_French_Empire). At this time there was a strong Gothic resurgence across Europe and in France; the associated architect was [Eugène Viollet-le-Duc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eug%C3%A8ne_Viollet-le-Duc). In the late 19th century, [Gustave Eiffel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustave_Eiffel) designed many bridges, such as the [Garabit viaduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garabit_viaduct), and remains one of the most influential bridge designers of his time, although he is best remembered for the [Eiffel Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eiffel_Tower).&#x20;

In the 20th century, French-Swiss architect [Le Corbusier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Corbusier) designed several buildings in France. More recently, French architects have combined both modern and old architectural styles. The [Louvre Pyramid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louvre_Pyramid) is an example of modern architecture added to an older building. The most difficult buildings to integrate within French cities are skyscrapers, as they are visible from afar. For instance, in Paris since 1977, new buildings had to be under 37 metres (121 ft).[^cite_note-340] A significant number of skyscrapers are located in the [La Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_D%C3%A9fense) financial district.[^cite_note-341] Other massive buildings that are a challenge to integrate into their environment are large bridges; an example of the way this has been done is the [Millau Viaduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millau_Viaduct). Some famous modern French architects include [Jean Nouvel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Nouvel), [Dominique Perrault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominique_Perrault), [Christian de Portzamparc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_de_Portzamparc) and [Paul Andreu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Andreu).&#x20;

### Literature and philosophy



[Victor Hugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Hugo), a French [Romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) writer and politician

The earliest [French literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_literature) dates from the Middle Ages, when there were several languages and dialects, and writers used their own spelling and grammar. Some authors of medieval texts, such as *[Tristan and Iseult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tristan_and_Iseult)* and *[Lancelot-Grail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancelot-Grail),* are unknown. Three famous medieval authors are [Chrétien de Troyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chr%C3%A9tien_de_Troyes), [Christine de Pizan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christine_de_Pizan) ([langue d'oïl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langues_d%27o%C3%AFl)), and Duke [William IX of Aquitaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_IX_of_Aquitaine) ([langue d'oc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occitan_language)). Much medieval [French poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_poetry) and literature was inspired by the legends of the [Carolingian cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matter_of_France), such as the *[Song of Roland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_of_Roland)* and the [chansons de geste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chansons_de_geste). The *Roman de Renart*, written in 1175 by Perrout de Saint Cloude, tells the story of the medieval character [Reynard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reynard_the_Fox) ('the Fox') and is another example of early French writing. An important 16th-century writer was [François Rabelais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Rabelais), who wrote five popular early [picaresque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picaresque) novels. Rabelais was also in regular communication with [Marguerite de Navarre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marguerite_de_Navarre), author of the *[Heptaméron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heptam%C3%A9ron)*.[^cite_note-342] [Michel de Montaigne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_de_Montaigne)'s most famous work, *[Essais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essays_\(Montaigne\))*, started a literary genre.[^cite_note-343]&#x20;

French literature and poetry flourished during the 18th and 19th centuries. [Denis Diderot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denis_Diderot) is best known as the main editor of the *[Encyclopédie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A9die)*, whose aim was to sum up all the knowledge of his century and to fight ignorance and [obscurantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obscurantism). [Charles Perrault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Perrault) was a prolific writer of children's fairy tales including *[Puss in Boots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puss_in_Boots)*, *[Cinderella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinderella)*, *[Sleeping Beauty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleeping_Beauty)* and *[Bluebeard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluebeard)*. At the start of the 19th century, [symbolist poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolism_\(movement\)) was an important movement, with poets such as [Charles Baudelaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Baudelaire), [Paul Verlaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Verlaine) and [Stéphane Mallarmé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St%C3%A9phane_Mallarm%C3%A9).[^cite_note-344]&#x20;

[Victor Hugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Hugo) is sometimes seen as "the greatest French writer of all time"[^cite_note-victor-345] for excelling in all literary genres. Hugo's verse has been compared to that of [Shakespeare](32897.md), [Dante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante_Alighieri) and [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer).[^cite_note-hugo-346] His novel *[Les Misérables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Mis%C3%A9rables)* is widely seen as one of the greatest novels ever written,[^cite_note-347] and *[The Hunchback of Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hunchback_of_Notre-Dame)* has remained immensely popular. Other major authors of the 19th century include [Alexandre Dumas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandre_Dumas) (*[The Three Musketeers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Three_Musketeers)* and *[The Count of Monte Cristo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Count_of_Monte_Cristo)*), [Jules Verne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Verne) (*[Twenty Thousand Leagues Under the Seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty_Thousand_Leagues_Under_the_Seas)*), [Émile Zola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89mile_Zola) (*[Les Rougon-Macquart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Rougon-Macquart)*), [Honoré de Balzac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honor%C3%A9_de_Balzac) (*[La Comédie humaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Com%C3%A9die_humaine)*), [Guy de Maupassant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_de_Maupassant), [Théophile Gautier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9ophile_Gautier) and [Stendhal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stendhal) (*[The Red and the Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Red_and_the_Black)*, *[The Charterhouse of Parma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Charterhouse_of_Parma)*).&#x20;

In the early 20th century France was a haven for literary freedom.[^cite_note-Beat_censors-348] Works banned for obscenity in the US, the UK and other Anglophone nations were published in France decades before they were available in the respective authors' home countries.[^cite_note-Beat_censors-348] The French were disinclined to punish literary figures for their writing, and prosecutions were rare.[^cite_note-Beat_censors-348] Important writers of the 20th century include [Marcel Proust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_Proust), [Louis-Ferdinand Céline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis-Ferdinand_C%C3%A9line), [Jean Cocteau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Cocteau), [Albert Camus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Camus), and [Jean-Paul Sartre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Paul_Sartre). [Antoine de Saint-Exupéry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_de_Saint-Exup%C3%A9ry) wrote *[The Little Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Little_Prince)*, which is one of the best selling books in history.[^cite_note-Patrick_Modiano-349][^cite_note-350]&#x20;

#### Philosophy

Medieval [French philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_philosophy) was dominated by [Scholasticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholasticism) until the emergence of [Humanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanism_in_France) in the Renaissance. [Modern philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_philosophy) began in the 17th century with [René Descartes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Descartes), [Blaise Pascal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaise_Pascal) and [Nicolas Malebranche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Malebranche). Descartes was the first [Western philosopher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_philosophy) since ancient times to attempt to build a philosophical system from the ground up rather than building on the work of predecessors.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352] Major philosophical contributions in the 18th century included [Voltaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voltaire), who came to embody the Enlightenment, and [Jean-Jacques Rousseau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Jacques_Rousseau), whose work highly influenced the French Revolution.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354] French philosophers made major contributions to the field in the 20th century including the [existentialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Existentialism) works of [Simone de Beauvoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simone_de_Beauvoir), Camus, and Sartre.[^cite_note-355] Other influential contributors include the moral and political works of [Simone Weil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simone_Weil), contributions to [structuralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structuralism) including from [Claude Lévi-Strauss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_L%C3%A9vi-Strauss) and the [post-structuralist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-structuralism) works by [Michel Foucault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Foucault).[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357]&#x20;

### Music

Main article: [Music of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_France)



[Claude Debussy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Debussy), a French composer

[French music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_France) experienced a golden age in the 17th century thanks to [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV), who employed talented musicians and composers in the royal court. Composers of this period include [Marc-Antoine Charpentier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc-Antoine_Charpentier), [François Couperin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Couperin), [Michel-Richard Delalande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel-Richard_Delalande), [Jean-Baptiste Lully](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Lully) and [Marin Marais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marin_Marais), all of them composers at the court. After the death of Louis XIV, French musical creation lost dynamism, but in the next century the music of [Jean-Philippe Rameau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Philippe_Rameau) achieved some prestige. Rameau became the dominant composer of [French opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_opera) and the leading French composer of the harpsichord.[^cite_note-358]&#x20;

In the field of [classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_music), France has produced notable composers such as [Gabriel Fauré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Faur%C3%A9), [Claude Debussy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Debussy), [Maurice Ravel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Ravel), and [Hector Berlioz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hector_Berlioz). Debussy and Ravel are the most prominent figures associated with [Impressionist music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionism_in_music). The two composers invented new musical forms[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362] and new sounds. Debussy was among the most influential composers of the late 19th and early 20th centuries, and his use of non-traditional scales and [chromaticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromaticism) influenced many composers who followed.[^cite_note-363] His music is noted for its sensory content and frequent usage of [atonality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atonality). [Erik Satie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_Satie) was a key member of the early-20th-century Parisian [avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant-garde). [Francis Poulenc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Poulenc)'s best-known works are his piano suite *[Trois mouvements perpétuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mouvements_perp%C3%A9tuels)* (1919), the ballet *[Les Biches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Biches)* (1923), the *[Concert champêtre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concert_champ%C3%AAtre)* (1928) for harpsichord and orchestra, the opera *[Dialogues des Carmélites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialogues_of_the_Carmelites)* (1957) and the *[Gloria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloria_\(Poulenc\))* (1959) for soprano, choir and orchestra. In the middle of the 20th century, [Maurice Ohana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Ohana), [Pierre Schaeffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Schaeffer) and [Pierre Boulez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Boulez) contributed to the evolution of [contemporary classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_classical_music).[^cite_note-364]&#x20;

French music then followed the rapid emergence of pop and rock music in the middle of the 20th century. Although English-speaking creations achieved popularity in the country, [French pop music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_popular_music), known as *[chanson française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chanson_fran%C3%A7aise)*, has also remained very popular. Among the most important French artists of the century are [Édith Piaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89dith_Piaf), [Georges Brassens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Brassens), [Léo Ferré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9o_Ferr%C3%A9), [Charles Aznavour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Aznavour) and [Serge Gainsbourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serge_Gainsbourg).[^cite_note-365] Modern pop music has seen the rise of popular [French hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_hip_hop), [French rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_rock), [techno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Techno)/[funk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk), and [turntablists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turntablist)/DJs. Although there are very few rock bands in France compared to English-speaking countries,[^cite_note-366] bands such as [Noir Désir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noir_D%C3%A9sir), [Mano Negra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mano_Negra_\(band\)), [Niagara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niagara_\(band\)), [Les Rita Mitsouko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Rita_Mitsouko) and more recently [Superbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superbus_\(band\)), [Phoenix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_\(French_band\)) and [Gojira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gojira_\(band\)),[^cite_note-frmusic-367] or [Shaka Ponk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaka_Ponk), have reached worldwide popularity.&#x20;

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_France)

Palme d'Or award in presentation case

A *[Palme d'Or](612946.md)* from the [Cannes Film Festival](101460.md), one of the "[Big Three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_festival)" film festivals alongside the [Venice Film Festival](32497.md) and [Berlin International Film Festival](18948039.md)

[^cite_note-368]

[^cite_note-369]

[^cite_note-370]

France has historical and strong links with [cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filmmaking), with two Frenchmen, Auguste and Louis Lumière (known as the [Lumière Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_and_Louis_Lumi%C3%A8re)) credited with creating cinema in 1895.[^cite_note-371] The world's first female filmmaker, [Alice Guy-Blaché](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Guy-Blach%C3%A9), was also from France.[^cite_note-372] Several important cinematic movements, including the late 1950s and 1960s [Nouvelle Vague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nouvelle_Vague), began in the country. It is noted for having a strong film industry, due in part to protections afforded by the government. France remains a leader in filmmaking, as of 2015 producing more films than any other European country.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-NYT_1995-02-28-374] The nation also hosts the [Cannes Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannes_Festival), one of the most important and famous film festivals in the world.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]&#x20;

Apart from its strong and innovative film tradition, France has also been a gathering spot for artists from across Europe and the world. For this reason, French cinema is sometimes intertwined with the cinema of foreign nations. Directors from nations such as Poland ([Roman Polanski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Polanski), [Krzysztof Kieślowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krzysztof_Kie%C5%9Blowski), [Andrzej Żuławski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrzej_%C5%BBu%C5%82awski)), Argentina ([Gaspar Noé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaspar_No%C3%A9), [Edgardo Cozarinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgardo_Cozarinsky)), Russia ([Alexandre Alexeieff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandre_Alexeieff), [Anatole Litvak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatole_Litvak)), Austria ([Michael Haneke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Haneke)) and Georgia ([Géla Babluani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9la_Babluani), [Otar Iosseliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otar_Iosseliani)) are prominent in the ranks of French cinema. Conversely, French directors have had prolific and influential careers in other countries, such as [Luc Besson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luc_Besson), [Jacques Tourneur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Tourneur) or [Francis Veber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Veber) in the [United States](418334.md). Although the French film market is dominated by Hollywood, France is the only nation in the world where American films make up the smallest share of total film revenues, at 50%, compared with 77% in Germany and 69% in Japan.[^cite_note-erudit-377] French films account for 35% of the total film revenues of France, which is the highest percentage of national film revenues in the developed world outside the United States, compared to 14% in Spain and 8% in the UK.[^cite_note-erudit-377] In 2013, France was the second-largest exporter of films in the world, after the United States.[^cite_note-unifrance.org-378]&#x20;

As part of its advocacy of [cultural exception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_exception), a political concept of treating culture differently from other commercial products,[^cite_note-379] France succeeded in convincing all EU members to refuse to include culture and audiovisuals in the list of liberalised sectors of the WTO in 1993.[^cite_note-380] This decision was confirmed in a vote by [UNESCO](21786641.md) in 2005.[^cite_note-381]&#x20;

### Fashion

Main article: [French fashion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_fashion)

Chanel's headquarters storefront window at the Place Vendôme Paris with awning

[Chanel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chanel)'s headquarters on [Place Vendôme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Vend%C3%B4me), Paris

Fashion has been an important industry and cultural export of France since the 17th century, and modern "[haute couture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haute_couture)" originated in Paris in the 1860s. Today, Paris, along with London, Milan, and New York City, is considered one of the world's [fashion capitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_capital), and the city is home or headquarters to many of the premier fashion houses. The expression Haute couture is, in France, a legally protected name, guaranteeing certain quality standards.&#x20;

The association of France with fashion and style (French: *la mode*) dates largely to the reign of [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV).[^cite_note-382] France renewed its dominance of the high fashion (French: *couture or haute couture*) industry in the years 1860–1960 through the establishment of the great [couturier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Couturier) houses such as [Chanel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chanel), [Dior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Dior_S.A.), and [Givenchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Givenchy). The French perfume industry is the world leader in its sector and is centred on the town of [Grasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grasse).[^cite_note-383]&#x20;

According to 2017 data compiled by [Deloitte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deloitte), [Louis Vuitton Moet Hennessey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Vuitton_Moet_Hennessey) (LVMH), a French brand, is the largest luxury company in the world by sales, selling more than twice the amount of its nearest competitor.[^cite_note-mode-384] Moreover, France also possesses 3 of the top 10 luxury goods companies by sales ([LVMH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LVMH), [Kering SA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kering_SA), [L'Oréal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%27Or%C3%A9al)), more than any other country in the world.[^cite_note-mode-384]&#x20;

### Media

Main articles: [Telecommunications in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_France) and [Censorship in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_France)



*[Le Figaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Figaro)* was founded in 1826 and is still considered a [newspaper of record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspaper_of_record).

[^cite_note-385]

In 2021, regional daily newspapers, such as *[Ouest-France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ouest-France)*, *[Sud Ouest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sud_Ouest_\(newspaper\))*, *[La Voix du Nord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Voix_du_Nord_\(daily\))*, *[Dauphiné Libéré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dauphin%C3%A9_Lib%C3%A9r%C3%A9)*, *[Le Télégramme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_T%C3%A9l%C3%A9gramme)*, and *[Le Progrès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Progr%C3%A8s)*, more than doubled the sales of national newspapers, such as *[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde)*, *[Le Figaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Figaro)*, *[L'Équipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%27%C3%89quipe)* (sports), *[Le Parisien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Parisien)*, and *[Les Echos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Echos_\(France\))* (finance). Free dailies, distributed in metropolitan centres, continue to increase their market share.[^cite_note-386] The sector of weekly magazines includes more than 400 specialised weekly magazines published in the country.[^cite_note-387]&#x20;

The most influential news magazines are the left-wing *[Le Nouvel Observateur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%27Obs)*, centrist *[L'Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%27Express_\(France\))* and right-wing *[Le Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Point)* (in 2009 more than 400,000 copies),[^cite_note-388] but the highest circulation numbers for weeklies are attained by TV magazines and by women's magazines, among them *[Marie Claire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marie_Claire)* and *[Elle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elle_\(magazine\))*, which have foreign versions. Influential weeklies also include investigative and satirical papers *[Le Canard Enchaîné](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Canard_Encha%C3%AEn%C3%A9)* and *[Charlie Hebdo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Hebdo)*, as well as *[Paris Match](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Match)*. As in most industrialised nations, the print media have been affected by a [severe crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspaper_crisis) with the rise of the internet. In 2008, the government launched a major initiative to help the sector reform and become financially independent,[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390] but in 2009 it had to give €600,000 to help the print media cope with the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), in addition to existing subsidies.[^cite_note-391] In 1974, after years of centralised monopoly on radio and television, the governmental agency [ORTF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ORTF) was split into several national institutions, but the three already-existing TV channels and four national radio stations[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-mediapol-393] remained under state control. It was only in 1981 that the government allowed free broadcasting in the territory.[^cite_note-mediapol-393]&#x20;

Some consider [hate speech laws in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_speech_laws_in_France) to be too broad or severe, undermining [freedom of speech](21401843.md).[^cite_note-394]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [French cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_cuisine)



[French wines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_wine) are usually made to accompany French cuisine.

Different regions have different styles. In the north, butter and cream are common ingredients, whereas [olive oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil) is more commonly used in the south.[^cite_note-395] Each region of France has traditional specialties: [cassoulet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassoulet) in the southwest, [choucroute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choucroute_garnie) in Alsace, [quiche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiche) in the [Lorraine region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorraine), [beef bourguignon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beef_bourguignon) in [Burgundy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burgundy), [Provençal](48503.md) [tapenade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapenade), etc. France is most famous for its [wines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_wine)[^cite_note-396] and [cheeses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_French_cheeses), which are often named for the territory where they are produced ([AOC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appellation_d%27origine_contr%C3%B4l%C3%A9e)).[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398] A meal typically consists of three courses, *entrée* ('starter'), *plat principal* ('main course'), and *fromage* ('cheese') or *dessert*, sometimes with a salad served before the cheese or dessert.&#x20;

French cuisine is also regarded as a key element of the [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life) and the attractiveness of France. A French publication, the *[Michelin Guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_Guide)*, awards *Michelin stars* for excellence to a select few establishments.[^cite_note-399][^cite_note-400] The acquisition or loss of a star can have dramatic effects on the success of a restaurant. By 2006, the *Michelin Guide* had awarded 620 stars to French restaurants.[^cite_note-401]&#x20;

In addition to its wine tradition, France is also a major producer of beer and rum. The three main French brewing regions are Alsace (60% of national production), Nord-Pas-de-Calais, and Lorraine. [French rum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_French_rums) is made in distilleries on islands in the Atlantic and Indian oceans.[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_France)

The peloton in the streets of Nice during the 2nd stage of the Tour de France on 30 August 2020

Starting in 1903, the [Tour de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_de_France) is the most prestigious of [Grands Tours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Tour_\(cycling\)), and the world's most famous cycling race.

[^cite_note-404]

France hosts "the world's biggest annual sporting event", the annual cycling race [Tour de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_de_France).[^cite_note-405] Other popular sports played in France include [football](10568.md), [judo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judo), [tennis](29773.md),[^cite_note-406] [rugby union](25405.md)[^cite_note-407] and [pétanque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A9tanque). France has hosted events such as the [1938](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938_FIFA_World_Cup) and [1998 FIFA World Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_FIFA_World_Cup),[^cite_note-408] the [2007 Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Rugby_World_Cup),[^cite_note-409] and the [2023 Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Rugby_World_Cup).[^cite_note-410] The country also hosted the [1960 European Nations' Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_European_Nations%27_Cup), [UEFA Euro 1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_1984), [UEFA Euro 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2016) and [2019 FIFA Women's World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_FIFA_Women%27s_World_Cup). The [Stade de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stade_de_France) in [Saint-Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Denis,_Seine-Saint-Denis) is France's largest stadium and was the venue for the 1998 FIFA World Cup and 2007 Rugby World Cup finals. Since 1923, the country has hosted the famous [24 Hours of Le Mans](1401596.md) sports car [endurance race](699018.md).[^cite_note-411] Several major tennis tournaments also take place in France, including the [Paris Masters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Masters) and the [French Open](147724.md), one of the four [Grand Slam](197638.md) tournaments. [Martial arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_arts) originating in France include [Savate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savate) and [Fencing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fencing). France will also host the [2031 FIBA Basketball World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2031_FIBA_Basketball_World_Cup), marking the first time the country has hosted a international basketball competition.&#x20;

France has a close association with the Modern [Olympic Games](22576.md); it was a French aristocrat, Baron [Pierre de Coubertin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_de_Coubertin), who suggested the Games' revival, at the end of the 19th century.[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-Olympic-413] Paris hosted the second Games [in 1900](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_Summer_Olympics),[^cite_note-414] and has hosted the Olympics on five further occasions: the [1924 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1924_Summer_Olympics), the [2024 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Summer_Olympics) both in Paris[^cite_note-Olympic-413] and three [Winter Games](34069.md) ([1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1924_Winter_Olympics) in [Chamonix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamonix), [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Winter_Olympics) in [Grenoble](50972.md) and [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Winter_Olympics) in [Albertville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albertville)) will host again in [2030](57658858.md) in the [French Alps](961317.md)[^cite_note-Olympic-413] France introduced Olympics for deaf people (Deaflympics) in [1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1924_Summer_Deaflympics).[^cite_note-415]&#x20;

Both the [national football team](250197.md), the [national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_men%27s_national_basketball_team) and the [national rugby union team](955656.md) are nicknamed "*Les Bleus*". Football is the most popular sport in France, with over 1,800,000 registered players and over 18,000 registered clubs.[^cite_note-416] [Rugby union](25405.md) is also popular, particularly in Paris and the southwest of France.[^cite_note-417] The national rugby union team has competed at every [Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_World_Cup); it also takes part in the annual [Six Nations Championship](26547.md). [Basketball ](3921.md) is another strong sport the France plays. They also competed nine times in the [FIBA Basketball World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Basketball_World_Cup).&#x20;

The French Open, also called Roland-Garros, is a major [tennis](29773.md) tournament held over two weeks between late May and early June at the [Stade Roland-Garros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stade_Roland_Garros) in Paris. It is the premier clay court tennis championship event in the world and the second of four annual [Grand Slam](197638.md) tournaments.[^cite_note-418]&#x20;

[^cite_note-3]: &#x20;French is the most widely spoken and is the sole official language in France. Although the [Constitution of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_France) does not officially recognise regional languages, it characterises them as "part of France's heritage".<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-1)</sup> The [European Charter for Regional or Minority Languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Charter_for_Regional_or_Minority_Languages) was signed by France in 1999, but has not yet been ratified. The Charter would apply to the following seven regional languages of France: [Basque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_language), [Breton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breton_language), [Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language), [Corsican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corsican_language), [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language) ([Western Flemish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Flemish) and Standard Dutch), [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language) ([dialects of German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_dialects), [Standard German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_German) and [regional language of Alsace-Moselle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alsatian_language)) and [Occitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occitan_language).<sup>[\[2\]](#cite_note-2)</sup>\
    In addition to France's regional languages, there are also various [immigrant languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_France#Immigrant_languages) spoken.
